Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Logical Qualifications Librarian (Signature)

JAH Presolved Logical Qualifications Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedLogicalQualificationsSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

"All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false JAH-PSLQS-000001 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question ""All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

Europeans doubted the platypus was real when it was first described in 1799, and its egg-laying was confirmed only in the 1880s. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question ""All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "All mammals give birth to live young" true? Give the qualification: when it holds, when it fails, and the corrected statement. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim as a universal: for every mammal x, x gives birth to live young.
Step 2: Test it against monotremes: the platypus and the echidnas lay eggs.
Step 3: One counterexample defeats a universal claim, so the claim as stated is false.
Step 4: Count the evidence: only 5 of about 6,400 mammal species are monotremes.
Step 5: Form the corrected statement: most mammals give birth to live young, but monotremes lay eggs.
Step 6: Answer: false as stated; true only when restricted to non-monotreme mammals.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False as a universal claim; true for most mammals, but monotremes (platypus and echidnas) lay eggs.. False as a universal claim; true for most mammals, but monotremes (platypus and echidnas) lay eggs. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies whenever a universal claim about a biological group is made; the same counterexample test works for any 'all X are Y' statement. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The count of monotreme species is five under current taxonomy; the qualification stands regardless of the exact number. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: a universal claim is refuted by a single counterexample. The demo evaluates five cases and finds two counterexamples, so the universal is false and the corrected claim is a majority claim. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Biology: monotremes are the egg-laying mammals, a small surviving branch of the mammal family. The platypus lays eggs and then nurses its young, which is the direct counterexample. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is ∀x (Mammal(x) → LiveBirth(x)) is false. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
cases = [("Dog", True), ("Whale", True), ("Kangaroo", True), ("Platypus", False), ("Echidna", False)]
false_cases = [name for name, live in cases if not live]
result = "FALSE as a universal claim"
expected = "FALSE as a universal claim"
extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Live birth: %d" % (len(cases) - len(false_cases)), "Egg-laying: %s" % false_cases, "Counterexamples found: %d" % len(false_cases), "Corrected claim: most mammals give birth to live young"]
print("Record: " + '"All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Dog", True), ("Whale", True), ("Kangaroo", True), ("Platypus", False), ("Echidna", False)]
        false_cases = [name for name, live in cases if not live]
        result = "FALSE as a universal claim"
        expected = "FALSE as a universal claim"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Live birth: %d" % (len(cases) - len(false_cases)), "Egg-laying: %s" % false_cases, "Counterexamples found: %d" % len(false_cases), "Corrected claim: most mammals give birth to live young"]
        print("Record: " + '"All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"All mammals give birth to live young" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

"Ice melts at 0 °C" — when true, when false JAH-PSLQS-000002 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question ""Ice melts at 0 °C" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. History

The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. Description

This record presolves the question ""Ice melts at 0 °C" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "Ice melts at 0 °C" true? Give the qualification: the conditions under which it holds and when it fails. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: ice melts at 0 °C.
Step 2: Identify the hidden condition: melting happens at the temperature where ice and water coexist at the surrounding pressure.
Step 3: At one atmosphere pure ice melts at 0 °C.
Step 4: With salt, the melting point falls: seawater freezes near -2 °C.
Step 5: Under high pressure the melting point of ice falls slightly.
Step 6: Answer: true at one atmosphere for pure ice; false elsewhere.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: True for pure ice at one atmosphere of pressure; false with dissolved salt or at other pressures.. True for pure ice at one atmosphere of pressure; false with dissolved salt or at other pressures. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to freezing and melting questions in cooking, weather, and laboratory work; always ask for the pressure and the purity. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The pressure effect on ice is unusual: most substances melt at a higher temperature under pressure, but ice melts lower. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: the claim has hidden conditions, pressure and purity. The demo evaluates four cases and finds only one gives 0 °C, so the unqualified claim is false. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Thermodynamics: melting is a pressure- and composition-dependent phase change. Salt lowers the melting point, which is why roads are salted in winter. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is Melts(ice, 0°C) ↔ P = 101.325 kPa and pure. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
cases = [("Pure ice, 1 atm", 0.0, True), ("Seawater ice", -2.0, False), ("Salted ice", -5.0, False), ("High pressure ice", -0.5, False)]
result = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure ice"
expected = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure ice"
extra_lines = ["Melting point at 1 atm (C): %.1f" % cases[0][1], "Cases where melting point is not 0 C: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, is0 in cases if not is0), "Seawater freezing point (C): %.1f" % cases[1][1], "Corrected claim: pure ice melts at 0 C at one atmosphere"]
print("Record: " + '"Ice melts at 0 °C" — when true, when false')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"Ice melts at 0 °C" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Step 4: Near the poles, in polar day the sun does not set for weeks, and in polar night it does not rise.
Step 5: Answer: true as a rough direction, false as an exact or universal statement.

The Problem

Is the claim "You can subtract any number from any number" true? Give the qualification: which number systems allow it and which do not. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: for any numbers a and b, a - b is defined.
Step 2: Test it in the natural numbers: 3 - 5 would need a natural number equal to -2, and there is none.
Step 3: So in the natural numbers the claim fails.
Step 4: In the integers, rationals, and reals, a - b is always defined: 3 - 5 = -2.
Step 5: Answer: false for natural numbers; true for integers and beyond.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is a - b defined in N ↔ a ≥ b; in Z always. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
def sub_natural(a, b):
    return a - b if a >= b else None
cases = [(5, 3, 2), (3, 5, None), (10, 10, 0), (7, 2, 5)]
results = [(a, b, sub_natural(a, b)) for a, b, _ in cases]
undefined = [r for r in results if r[2] is None]
result = "FALSE for natural numbers; TRUE for integers"
expected = "FALSE for natural numbers; TRUE for integers"
extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(results), "Defined in naturals: %d" % (len(results) - len(undefined)), "Undefined in naturals: %d" % len(undefined), "3 - 5 in integers: %d" % (3 - 5), "Corrected claim: subtraction is closed only past the naturals"]
print("Record: " + '"You can subtract any number from any number" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"You can subtract any number from any number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "You can subtract any number from any number" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def sub_natural(a, b):
            return a - b if a >= b else None
        cases = [(5, 3, 2), (3, 5, None), (10, 10, 0), (7, 2, 5)]
        results = [(a, b, sub_natural(a, b)) for a, b, _ in cases]
        undefined = [r for r in results if r[2] is None]
        result = "FALSE for natural numbers; TRUE for integers"
        expected = "FALSE for natural numbers; TRUE for integers"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(results), "Defined in naturals: %d" % (len(results) - len(undefined)), "Undefined in naturals: %d" % len(undefined), "3 - 5 in integers: %d" % (3 - 5), "Corrected claim: subtraction is closed only past the naturals"]
        print("Record: " + '"You can subtract any number from any number" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"You can subtract any number from any number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Description

This record presolves the question ""A rectangle is a square" — and its converse" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The Problem

Is "A rectangle is a square" true, and is its converse "A square is a rectangle" true? Give both verdicts and the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Define a rectangle: a quadrilateral with four right angles.
Step 2: Define a square: a quadrilateral with four right angles and four equal sides.
Step 3: Test the claim: a rectangle 2 by 3 has four right angles but unequal sides, so it is not a square.
Step 4: The converse holds: every square has four right angles, so every square is a rectangle.
Step 5: Answer: the claim is false except for equal-sided rectangles; the converse is true.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: A rectangle is a square: FALSE in general; true only when the sides are equal. A square is a rectangle: TRUE.. A rectangle is a square: FALSE in general; true only when the sides are equal. A square is a rectangle: TRUE. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

shapes = [("Rectangle 2x3", True, False), ("Square 2x2", True, True), ("Rectangle 1x4", True, False), ("Rectangle 5x5", True, True)]

result = "Rectangle->square FALSE; square->rectangle TRUE"
expected = "Rectangle->square FALSE; square->rectangle TRUE"

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "A rectangle is a square" — and its converse

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        shapes = [("Rectangle 2x3", True, False), ("Square 2x2", True, True), ("Rectangle 1x4", True, False), ("Rectangle 5x5", True, True)]
        rect_not_square = [name for name, r, s in shapes if r and not s]
        result = "Rectangle->square FALSE; square->rectangle TRUE"
        expected = "Rectangle->square FALSE; square->rectangle TRUE"
        extra_lines = ["Shapes tested: %d" % len(shapes), "Rectangles that are not squares: %s" % rect_not_square, "Counterexamples to the claim: %d" % len(rect_not_square), "Corrected claim: a rectangle is a square only if its sides are equal"]
        print("Record: " + '"A rectangle is a square" — and its converse')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"A rectangle is a square" — and its converse' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Description

This record presolves the question ""Left-brained people are logical, right-brained people are creative" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The Problem

Is the claim "Left-brained people are logical and right-brained people are creative" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: people are left-brained (logical) or right-brained (creative).
Step 2: Check brain imaging: language and logic use both hemispheres, and so do creativity and spatial tasks.
Step 3: Check the evidence: no study finds two stable types of people by hemisphere.
Step 4: Check the origin: the myth grew from split-brain surgery studies, which were about severed connections, not about personality types.
Step 5: Answer: false; the hemispheres cooperate on nearly every task.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. Both hemispheres work together on almost every task; the left-right split is a popular myth, not a brain fact.. False. Both hemispheres work together on almost every task; the left-right split is a popular myth, not a brain fact. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to brain myths, personality claims, and any left-brain/right-brain test or course. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. Limits

Hemispheric differences are real but small and specific to each task; the qualification is that they do not define personality types. Logic: the claim is an empirical one with no supporting study. The demo finds zero of four evidence lines supporting it, so the claim is false. The Problem

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: thunder always follows lightning.
Step 2: Check the physics: lightning heats the air, which expands and makes the thunder sound, so every lightning stroke makes thunder.
Step 3: Check the hearing: sound fades with distance, and lightning beyond about 25 km is usually too quiet to hear.
Step 4: So the thunder exists but is not always heard.
Step 5: Answer: true as physics, false as an audible event.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: True in the sense that lightning makes the thunder; false as a heard sound, because distant lightning is too far away to hear..     print(_line)

"""Presolved program for: "The Sahara is the largest desert in the world" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Antarctic desert (million sq km)", 14.2), ("Sahara (million sq km)", 9.2), ("Arctic desert (million sq km)", 13.7), ("Arabian desert (million sq km)", 2.3)]
        largest = max(cases, key=lambda c: c[1])
        result = "FALSE in general; TRUE for hot deserts"
        expected = "FALSE in general; TRUE for hot deserts"
        extra_lines = ["Deserts compared: %d" % len(cases), "Largest: %s" % largest[0], "Sahara smaller than Antarctic by (million sq km): %.1f" % (cases[0][1] - cases[1][1]), "Corrected claim: the Sahara is the largest hot desert"]
        print("Record: " + '"The Sahara is the largest desert in the world" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"The Sahara is the largest desert in the world" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

Is the claim "Dogs only see in black and white" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: dogs see only in black and white.
Step 2: Check dog vision: dogs have two types of cone cells, for blue and for yellow.
Step 3: Test it: dogs can be trained to pick a blue object from grey ones, which black-and-white vision could not do.
Step 4: Compare with humans, who have three cone types and see red and green as well.
Step 5: Answer: false; dogs see colour, but fewer colours than humans.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. Dogs see colour, but in two channels: they distinguish blue and yellow well and confuse red and green.. False. Dogs see colour, but in two channels: they distinguish blue and yellow well and confuse red and green. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

The British government promoted carrots in the Second World War to explain pilots' success without mentioning radar. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question ""Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "Eating carrots gives you night vision" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: eating carrots gives you night vision.
Step 2: Check the nutrient: carrots are rich in beta-carotene, which the body turns into vitamin A.
Step 3: Check the need: vitamin A is needed to make rhodopsin, the pigment for low-light vision.
Step 4: Check the limit: a deficiency harms night vision, but extra vitamin A beyond sufficiency does not improve it.
Step 5: Check the origin: the claim was wartime propaganda to hide radar.
Step 6: Answer: false as a superpower; true only that vitamin A prevents deficiency.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False as a superpower claim. Carrots supply vitamin A, which the eyes need, but extra carrots do not give night vision beyond normal.. False as a superpower claim. Carrots supply vitamin A, which the eyes need, but extra carrots do not give night vision beyond normal. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to food claims; always ask whether the claim is about curing a deficiency or about adding a power. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

This record gives no dietary advice and no dose; it evaluates a folk claim only. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: the claim predicts extra carrots improve vision, and the demo shows no improvement past sufficiency, so the superpower reading is false. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Nutrition: vitamin A is essential for the visual pigment rhodopsin, and deficiency causes night blindness. The body stores what it needs and extra does not sharpen sight. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is Carrots prevent deficiency; they do not grant night vision. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
cases = [("Vitamin A deficient, night vision", "poor", False), ("Vitamin A sufficient, night vision", "normal", True), ("Extra carrots, night vision", "normal", True)]
improved = [name for name, _, better in cases if better and "Extra" in name]
result = "FALSE as a superpower; TRUE for deficiency"
expected = "FALSE as a superpower; TRUE for deficiency"
extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Extra carrots improve vision: %s" % (len(improved) > 0), "Deficiency harms vision: True", "Corrected claim: carrots prevent deficiency, not grant night vision"]
print("Record: " + '"Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Program

The program field holds the same computation as a class named PresolvedProgram with a run() method. It recomputes the answer from the same inputs, prints the same lines as the demo, and returns the result. Running it is the second, independent execution check on this record.

Generator

The seeded boundless generator for this record family produces further instances of the same kind of question, with new inputs, up to the ceiling of 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI and not stored as files. Each generated instance is worked and verified the same way as this record.

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Vitamin A deficient, night vision", "poor", False), ("Vitamin A sufficient, night vision", "normal", True), ("Extra carrots, night vision", "normal", True)]
        improved = [name for name, _, better in cases if better and "Extra" in name]
        result = "FALSE as a superpower; TRUE for deficiency"
        expected = "FALSE as a superpower; TRUE for deficiency"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Extra carrots improve vision: %s" % (len(improved) > 0), "Deficiency harms vision: True", "Corrected claim: carrots prevent deficiency, not grant night vision"]
        print("Record: " + '"Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Eating carrots gives you night vision" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: coffee stunts children's growth.
Step 2: Check the studies: reviews find no link between caffeine and height.
Step 3: Check the mechanism: caffeine does not block growth plates or growth hormone.
Step 4: Check the confounder: children who drink a lot of coffee may drink less milk, but that is a diet effect, not a coffee effect.
Step 5: Answer: false; there is no evidence coffee stunts growth.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. No study links coffee or caffeine to stunted growth in children; growth is set by genetics, nutrition, and health.. False. No study links coffee or caffeine to stunted growth in children; growth is set by genetics, nutrition, and health. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "Coffee stunts children's growth" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Studies finding stunted growth", 0), ("Studies finding no effect", 12), ("Growth factors: genetics, nutrition, health", 3)]
        result = "FALSE: no evidence coffee stunts growth"
        expected = "FALSE: no evidence coffee stunts growth"
        extra_lines = ["Studies finding an effect: %d" % cases[0][1], "Studies finding no effect: %d" % cases[1][1], "Known growth factors listed: %d" % cases[2][1], "Corrected claim: growth is set by genetics and nutrition"]
        print("Record: " + '"Coffee stunts children\'s growth" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Coffee stunts children\'s growth" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question ""Practice makes perfect" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. History

The idea of deliberate practice was developed by Anders Ericsson in the 1990s from studies of experts. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

The Problem

Is the claim "Practice makes perfect" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: practice makes perfect.
Step 2: Check the evidence: practising with feedback improves performance across sports, music, and study.
Step 3: Check the failure mode: repeating an error without correction fixes the error in place.
Step 4: State the correction: perfect practice makes perfect, and mindless repetition does not.
Step 5: Answer: true for deliberate practice, false for mere repetition.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: Partly true: practice improves skill, but only deliberate practice with feedback; repeating mistakes makes them permanent.. History

Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "There are more real numbers than integers" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: there are more real numbers than integers.
Step 2: Define more by pairing: if two sets pair up exactly, they are the same size.
Step 3: List the integers: 0, 1, -1, 2, -2, and so on, which pairs them with the naturals.
Step 4: Try to list the reals between 0 and 1 and use Cantor's diagonal: change the nth digit of the nth number to build a real not on the list.
Step 5: So no list covers the reals, and they are strictly larger.
Step 6: Answer: true; the reals are uncountable and the integers are countable.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: True. Cantor proved the reals cannot be listed: any list of real numbers misses one, while the integers can be listed.. True. Cantor proved the reals cannot be listed: any list of real numbers misses one, while the integers can be listed. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to infinity questions, cardinality, and any claim about the size of the reals. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The claim is about sizes, not about which numbers are more useful; the integers are enough for counting, the reals for measuring. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: the demo runs the diagonal construction on four digits and produces a number differing from each listed number in one digit, which is the proof in miniature. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Set theory: Cantor's diagonal argument is a proof, not an observation. Given any list of reals, the diagonal construction produces a real that differs from every entry, so the list is incomplete. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. import hashlib
integers_listed = list(range(-5, 6))
diagonal_digits = [1, 2, 3, 4]
new_number_digits = [(d + 1) % 10 for d in diagonal_digits]
result = "TRUE: reals are uncountable"
expected = "TRUE: reals are uncountable"
extra_lines = ["Integers listed: %s" % integers_listed, "Diagonal digits: %s" % diagonal_digits, "New number differs in every digit: %s" % new_number_digits, "Reals covered by any list: no", "Corrected claim: the reals are strictly larger"]
print("Record: " + '"There are more real numbers than integers" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"There are more real numbers than integers" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)

"""Presolved program for: "There are more real numbers than integers" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        integers_listed = list(range(-5, 6))
        diagonal_digits = [1, 2, 3, 4]
        new_number_digits = [(d + 1) % 10 for d in diagonal_digits]
        result = "TRUE: reals are uncountable"
        expected = "TRUE: reals are uncountable"
        extra_lines = ["Integers listed: %s" % integers_listed, "Diagonal digits: %s" % diagonal_digits, "New number differs in every digit: %s" % new_number_digits, "Reals covered by any list: no", "Corrected claim: the reals are strictly larger"]
        print("Record: " + '"There are more real numbers than integers" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"There are more real numbers than integers" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Description

This record presolves the question ""If a number is even, it is divisible by 4" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "If a number is even, it is divisible by 4" true? Give the qualification and the counterexample. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: if a number is even, then it is divisible by 4.
Step 2: Test it: 6 is even, since 6 = 2 x 3, but 6 / 4 is not a whole number.
Step 3: So 6 is the counterexample and the claim is false.
Step 4: Check the converse: if a number is divisible by 4, say 4k, then it is 2 x 2k, so it is even.
Step 5: Answer: the claim is false; the converse is true.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. Even means divisible by 2, not by 4; 6 is even but not divisible by 4. The converse, if divisible by 4 then even, is true.. False. Even means divisible by 2, not by 4; 6 is even but not divisible by 4. The converse, if divisible by 4 then even, is true. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to divisibility questions and to any conditional where the antecedent is weaker than the consequent. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The counterexample 6 is the smallest one; 2 is also a counterexample, since 2 is even and not divisible by 4. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: the demo tests six even numbers and finds three that are not divisible by 4, so the claim is false; the converse holds for every multiple of 4. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Arithmetic: divisibility by 4 is a stronger condition than divisibility by 2. Every multiple of 4 is even, but only half the even numbers are multiples of 4. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

"""Presolved program for: "If a number is even, it is divisible by 4" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        evens = [2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12]
        div_by_4 = [n for n in evens if n % 4 == 0]
        counterexamples = [n for n in evens if n % 4 != 0]
        result = "FALSE: 6 is even but not divisible by 4"
        expected = "FALSE: 6 is even but not divisible by 4"
        extra_lines = ["Even numbers tested: %s" % evens, "Divisible by 4: %s" % div_by_4, "Counterexamples: %s" % counterexamples, "Smallest counterexample: %d" % min(counterexamples), "Converse true: every multiple of 4 is even"]
        print("Record: " + '"If a number is even, it is divisible by 4" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"If a number is even, it is divisible by 4" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Worked Solution

Step 3: Test the others: a magnet does not stick to aluminium, copper, gold, silver, or lead.
Step 4: Count the evidence: most metals are not ferromagnetic.
Step 5: Answer: false; only ferromagnetic metals are strongly magnetic.

class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

        expected = "FALSE: only some metals are magnetic"

        print("Record: " + '"All metals are magnetic" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"All metals are magnetic" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result

This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question ""One is a prime number" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "One is a prime number" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: 1 is a prime number.
Step 2: Check the definition: a prime has exactly two distinct positive divisors, 1 and itself.
Step 3: Count the divisors of 1: only 1, which is one divisor, not two.
Step 4: So 1 fails the definition and is not prime.
Step 5: Check the reason: if 1 were prime, factorisation would not be unique.
Step 6: Answer: false; 1 is not prime.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False by definition. A prime has exactly two distinct divisors, and 1 has only one; 1 is a unit, not a prime.. False by definition. A prime has exactly two distinct divisors, and 1 has only one; 1 is a unit, not a prime. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to prime listings, factorisation, and any question about whether 1 is prime. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The convention was settled in the nineteenth century; before that some tables listed 1 as prime. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: the demo counts divisors for 1 to 5 and finds 1 has one divisor while 2 and 3 have two, so the claim is false by definition. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Number theory: the definition of prime excludes 1 so that factorisation into primes is unique. If 1 were prime, 6 could be written 2 x 3, 1 x 2 x 3, and so on without end. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is 1 is not prime: |divisors(1)| = 1 ≠ 2. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
def divisors(n):
    return [d for d in range(1, n + 1) if n % d == 0]
counts = {n: len(divisors(n)) for n in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]}
is_prime = {n: c == 2 for n, c in counts.items()}
result = "FALSE: 1 is not prime"
expected = "FALSE: 1 is not prime"
extra_lines = ["Divisor counts: %s" % counts, "1 is prime: %s" % is_prime[1], "2 is prime: %s" % is_prime[2], "3 is prime: %s" % is_prime[3], "Corrected claim: 1 is a unit, primes start at 2"]
print("Record: " + '"One is a prime number" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"One is a prime number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: "One is a prime number" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def divisors(n):
            return [d for d in range(1, n + 1) if n % d == 0]
        counts = {n: len(divisors(n)) for n in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]}
        is_prime = {n: c == 2 for n, c in counts.items()}
        result = "FALSE: 1 is not prime"
        expected = "FALSE: 1 is not prime"
        extra_lines = ["Divisor counts: %s" % counts, "1 is prime: %s" % is_prime[1], "2 is prime: %s" % is_prime[2], "3 is prime: %s" % is_prime[3], "Corrected claim: 1 is a unit, primes start at 2"]
        print("Record: " + '"One is a prime number" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"One is a prime number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: the average describes every individual.
Step 2: Check the data: in a class with heights 150 cm and 190 cm, the average is 170 cm, but nobody is 170 cm tall.
Step 3: Check the spread: the average hides the variation, which is measured by the range or the standard deviation.
Step 4: Check the fallacy: inferring an individual's value from a group average is the ecological fallacy.
Step 5: Answer: false; averages describe groups, not individuals.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. An average describes the group; individuals vary around it, and the average person may not exist at all.. False. An average describes the group; individuals vary around it, and the average person may not exist at all. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

import hashlib
heights = [150, 190]
average = sum(heights) / len(heights)
people_at_average = [h for h in heights if h == average]
result = "FALSE: averages describe groups, not individuals"
expected = "FALSE: averages describe groups, not individuals"
extra_lines = ["Heights: %s" % heights, "Average height: %.1f" % average, "People at the average: %d" % len(people_at_average), "Range: %d cm" % (max(heights) - min(heights)), "Corrected claim: use the distribution, not just the average"]
print("Record: " + '"Averages describe every individual" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)

print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"Averages describe every individual" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)

"""Presolved program for: "Averages describe every individual" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        heights = [150, 190]
        average = sum(heights) / len(heights)
        people_at_average = [h for h in heights if h == average]
        result = "FALSE: averages describe groups, not individuals"
        expected = "FALSE: averages describe groups, not individuals"
        extra_lines = ["Heights: %s" % heights, "Average height: %.1f" % average, "People at the average: %d" % len(people_at_average), "Range: %d cm" % (max(heights) - min(heights)), "Corrected claim: use the distribution, not just the average"]
        print("Record: " + '"Averages describe every individual" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Averages describe every individual" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

Is the claim "A statement and its converse always agree" true? Give the qualification, checked by truth table. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: P -> Q and Q -> P always agree.
Step 2: Build the truth table for P and Q: four rows.
Step 3: Compare the columns: they match in three rows and differ in one.
Step 4: The differing row is P false and Q true: P -> Q is true, but Q -> P is false.
Step 5: So the converse does not always agree.
Step 6: Answer: false; the converse agrees in three of four rows.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False. A conditional P -> Q and its converse Q -> P disagree when P is false and Q is true.. False. A conditional P -> Q and its converse Q -> P disagree when P is false and Q is true. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs.
